My usual things—art class, morning walks—they haven’t really helped the way they used to.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I’ve had rough patches before, but I don’t know if I’d call them like this, or if they lasted two weeks.\n\nDoctor: When something good happens, does your mood actually lift for a while?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… but it doesn’t last much. Most mornings I still wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like everything’s kind of drained out before I even get going.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really been tracking it. I guess nothing that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I’ll get stuck on small things at work, like if a sign wasn’t straight or someone I’m training seemed confused, and then I start thinking I’m just… not any good at being in charge.\n\nDoctor: When you’re away from work for a day or two, do you feel noticeably less depressed?\n\nPatient: Not really. Even if I’m off for a day or two, most mornings I still wake up with that heavy, gray feeling… like the color’s drained out before I even get dressed.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, were you generally feeling down or low most of the time for years?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say that. I’ve had rough stretches, sure, but I don’t think it was most of the time for years.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. Little decisions take longer than they should. Like at work, deciding which endcap to reset first, or even at home picking out a blouse—I’ll just stand there kind of foggy, second-guessing myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not a lot, I don’t think. I get tense sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as being on edge all the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
